=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        stack = []
        res = 0
        arr = [float('-inf')] + arr + [float('-inf')]
        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                res += arr[cur] * (i - cur) * (cur - stack[-1]
            stack.append(i)
        return res % (10**9 + 7)

        for i, num in enumerate(arr):

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

```
arr = [0] + arr + [0]
stack = [0]
res = 0
for i in range(1, len(arr)):
    while arr[stack[-1]] > arr[i]:
        cur = stack.pop()
        res += arr[cur] * (i - cur) * (cur - stack[-1])
    stack.append(i)
```
This is exactly our code with -inf. It uses `>` and sentinels. It counts duplicates as leftmost. Yes.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        stack = []
        res = 0
        arr = [float('-inf')] + arr + [float('-inf')]
        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                res += arr[cur] * (i - cur) * (cur - stack[-1])
            stack.append(i)
        return res % (10**9 + 7)

We think no. But let's be absolutely certain by comparing with known solution. Known solution for sum of subarray minimums:
```
class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] >= x:
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                right = i
                res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (right - cur)
            stack.append(i)
        while stack:
            cur = stack.pop()
            left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
            right = len(arr)
            res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (right - cur)
        return res % MOD
```
This uses `>=` and counts duplicates as rightmost. Our code uses sentinels and `>`, equivalent to leftmost. Let's verify with arr [1,2,1] using known `>=` would produce same total. Good.

Let's maybe derive with duplicates formally:
For each subarray, if multiple minima, our algorithm assigns to the leftmost minimum. For an element at index cur, it is the leftmost minimum of a subarray if:
- No element smaller than arr[cur] in subarray.
- No equal element to the left of cur within subarray.
Thus left boundary is previous index with value <= arr[cur] (previous smaller or equal). Right boundary is next index with value < arr[cur] (next strictly smaller). Our stack with `>` popping gives exactly previous <= and next <. Count formula. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        stack = []
        res = 0
        arr = [float('-inf')] + arr + [float('-inf')]
        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                res += arr[cur] * (i - cur) * (cur - stack[-1])
            stack.append(i)
        return res % (10**9 + 7)
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        stack = []
        res = 0
        arr = [float('-inf')] + arr + [float('-inf')]
        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                res += arr[cur] * (i - cur) * (cur - stack[-1])
            stack.append(i)
        return res % (10**9 + 7)
